ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e277	Let me begin by thanking the Committee for a good cooperation in dealing with this great and important communication. It is also apparent from a long statement from the Committee that there have been many opinions and disagreements. It is positive in such a big and important matter as the opportunity message is. It's important when we're going to discuss this, to start by defining whether all the inequality is wrong. There are differences that result in free choice, so simply in a family, for example. have chosen to go into a position, move to the country, work less and be more with their children, while others choose to stay in the middle of the city, work a lot and thus also have higher income. There's inequality between these families, but there's inequality as a result of two families taking free choice from the life they want to live. There are also inequalities that arise as a result of political choices that you want to make. When there was a refugee crisis in Europe, there was an agreement here on the Stortinget where the Stortinget said our country would accept 8 000 Syrian quota refugees. There are many good, high-ranking, right arguments that a rich country like Norway does, but the inequality in Norway does come up somewhat when the 8 000 people come to the country. When we had a financial crisis in 2008, hundreds of billions of dollars disappeared at Oslo Exchange. The inequality went down in Norway, but no one got better, so inequality is not the only parameter we can measure from if things go well or bad. That doesn't mean that big inequality isn't a problem if, for example, The inequality becomes too great, as can be seen in countries like the United States, where management wages gallop from farms and become very high compared to the incomes of ordinary people, who are lagging behind in wage developments, or if the inequalities are not the result of different choices but of different possibilities. There are countries where children go to elite schools, given that they have the right background, while others go to other schools. And then the inequality is going to be huge. It's not a development we want in Norway, and fortunately it doesn't. The situation in our country is – if you include our welfare systems – that we have the lowest inequality in the whole EEA area. That's positive, and that's something we're going to take care of. There are several reasons why the inequality in Norway is stablely low and has been so for many years. Firstly, we have a progressive tax system. The 10 pst. with the highest personal tax in Norway paying almost 40 pst. of the total taxes on the income on the persons of the country. The 10 pst. the richest paid 38.8 pst. of all personal taxes in 2017. But tax is not just redistribution, our tax system has many purposes – we're going to create jobs, we're going to prevent pollution, it's going to pay off for more jobs. That's why there's a lot of reasons underpinning our tax system. Therefore, the second part of the success factor in Norway is not just that the tax system is progressive, but what we use the tax revenue on. The fact that we have free education for everyone in Norway is very, very disparate. The fact that we have free health services in Norway for those who need it, when they need it, is very, very redistributive. In addition, we have arrangements for those who have low incomes, which means that some of the tax money is also targeted at those who need it the most. 85 pst. the increase in the number of children in families with long-term low incomes is in immigrant families. It is not an excuse, and it is not an excuse, but it is a very important factor to bring with it if the right measures are to be taken to prevent the inequality in Norway from growing. So what do we do with this? In 2019, the government implemented a number of measures to benefit children and families with children, such as: free of charge at kindergartens also for 2-year-olds in low-income families. This government has put in place a moderating mechanism to ensure that no families in Norway use more than 6 pst. of his or her income to kindergarten. It's very redistributive. Child welfare has increased, housing support has been eaten and targeted at the children’s families concerned, and the follow-up and pilot schemes for young people and increased allocation of area interventions in vulnerable urban areas have been extended. Another group that we know has been represented in the statistics on those with the lowest income is the country's pensioners. That is why, over the years that today's four government parties have been responsible for the country, we have increased the minimum pension by NOK 12 000. The proportion of old-age pensioners with persistent low incomes has fallen from below 30 p.p. at the end of the 1990s to 10 pst. in the period now. All of this is important to raise those who have been sitting at the bottom of the table for a long time. Nevertheless, the three main measures to prevent increasing inequality are employment, education and good integration. This government, in addition to pursuing a policy that has led to record low unemployment and increasing employment in Norway, has not taken it easy because it is also important that more of those outside the labour market come in. For this reason, an inclusion qualification has been initiated which allows more people with reduced working capacity or gaps in the CV to enter the labour market. 5 pst. of new posts in the State shall be made by people with holes in the CV. We have a requirement for compulsory activity for social welfare recipients, and it is now increasing to a age of 40, but we also see that there is a drop in the number of young people receiving social economic assistance. The youth initiatives, which are now established throughout the country, also ensure that several of the people who are having trouble getting into their first job are now getting it. The second is education, and here I mean there's a political bridge between the right and the left. One of the major paradoxes with Norway has been that over many years we have had a school that has not only reproduced but also reinforced social differences. The background and length of your parent’s education have been very important for how well you do it yourself at school. That is why it was absolutely essential that the then bourgeois majority actually, at the beginning of the 2000s, put in place the Knowledge promise, which was about just lifting those who needed it the most. After this important reform has been implemented, the proportion of students below the critical limit in grade 4 reading has gone from 10 pst. to 2 pst. It's equalizing social differences. There is much to be done in the field of integration and much still needs to be done. That is why this government has launched a series of measures to ensure that the proportion of immigrants we take into the country, such as refugees and asylum seekers, is on such a scale that we are in fact able to integrate them. But in addition, we also make some demands, e.g. that a person must take Norwegian training to receive social assistance, that short-term young people should receive a better organised offer with combination classes at secondary school, so that several complete and remain. With increased free core time in the kindergarten, which was adopted in the autumn of 2018, the total number of children who receive this offer – and many of them are children of immigrants – will have ranged from 11 300 to 45 100. However, let me end by mentioning some of the things that may be the main reason why the differences in Norway are small. That's our social model. It's e.g. that we have responsible parties who ensure that when the industry goes down, the workers make sure that they do not require more than that the companies survive that period, but that the cooperation between the parties also ensures that when the companies are doing well, the employees get their share of the success and profits. This avoids what you can see in many other countries. By the fact that we have a wage formation that is not about the fact that we in Stortinget sit and adopt a minimum wage, as you can see in other countries, which does not become a floor, but often a thank you, we have confidence that the parties, together with the state, will find good solutions for employees and employers across the country. It's also the case that we have institutions that are highly trusted, we have low corruption, people cooperate and trust each other – and in that way the inequalities are evened out. The debate we are going to have today on a matter on which the Finance Committee has worked a great deal, and which is the result of a communication from the government to the Stortinget, is important, because these results have not come of their own, and there is no law of nature for them to continue. But we also need to be clear about what are the most important measures to ensure that the inequality remains low in Norway, the lowest in the EEA area, and that is our social model, that is our education system, it is redistributive taxes, and it is a welfare society that gives to those who need it, when they need it.

But it has also increased the inequalities, and it shows some of the dilemmas we face when we make changes. In the short term, inequalities can increase – in the long term, hopefully, more jobs are created here at home. Although the Labour Party leader did not believe in it for some time in Political Quarter, according to the SSB, this increases the inequality more than cut in the wealth tax. And to take the property tax: if you are completely indifferent to whether Norwegian companies have foreign or Norwegian owners, it is entirely possible to defend a tax on wealth. If you think that foreign owners are sitting through the storm, as Norwegian owners of businesses and jobs do when it blows bad, you can undoubtedly defend it, even if you bottle jobs for capital to pay the tax. But it's not like that. It means something if the corner stone company is owned by Norwegian owners with Norwegian patriotic capital, or if they lose the fight against foreigners with tax advantages and have to sell the companies to them. The fact that, in particular, the Centre Party, which is otherwise occupied by Norwegian ownership, is hot defenders of this tax, which favours foreigners over Norwegian and Norwegian owners, is surprising. It is well possible that cuts in property taxes lead to an increase in inequality there and then, but it is and has been right to do so, just as it has been with corporate taxes. Work is the way out of poverty and much of the solution to income inequality. Not being at work over time reduces the living conditions, the health and the quality of life of individuals. This government has made a strong effort to get people to work, and we now see the results. The registered unemployment rate is the lowest in 10 years and employment is increasing. It is also very gratifying that the employment rate is increasing after several years of decline. In Norway, we have a well-functioning labour market and responsible parties on both the employee and employer sides. It has also increased the median pre-tax income by 60 pst in the last 30 years. This has benefited everyone. However, there are some danger signals we're going to take seriously. Technological developments can lead to fewer jobs in the short term and to the fact that those with the least formal education will have the most difficulty in finding work. There's a trend we've seen in the last decades that there'll be fewer and fewer jobs for those with little or no schooling beyond primary school. That is why the education system is crucial to getting more people into the labour market and to securing them income and a decent life and getting a society of small differences. More are now completing upper secondary education than six years ago. After the introduction of the absenteeism limit, which was opposed by several opposition parties, several hours are involved. It makes the danger of falling off become smaller. That is why it is worrying to, for example: The centre party warns against the absenteeism limit. It is ‘freaky and harmful to health’, writes the Senterungdom on its websites. The AUF went out in the previous election campaign and demanded that the first thing an Labour Party-led government should do was remove the absence limit. The most important thing for the Youth Party was to scrap away what makes more students in school now than before. The greatest danger of falling from upper secondary education is that of boys in vocational education. One of the biggest pitfalls is that they don't get apprenticeship space. Therefore, unlike what the red-green government did, the majority of the population have increased the apprenticeship grant, so that more companies find it profitable to take in apprentices. Together with the demand for apprentices to win public tenders, early efforts, more real science and more and better teachers, this will lead to more completion of the school. But to say that you wind up the universal welfare state step by step – there's no way! So this is said to be a government and a large majority who have invited to an inclusion exercise where joint efforts by public and private actors are to have more disabled people or holes in the CV into regular, regular jobs. It is a government that has simplified the pay subsidy scheme, so that more people will use it, and that has increased the appropriations for individual job support and the number of permanent job placements. It is a government that has greatly increased the appropriations for parental support, child protection, anti-drug and psychiatry work, and for the fight against violence and abuse, which has given more in education grants to young people in low-income families, who have only talked about the red-green parties for eight years, and given students eleven months of study funding, and who have more than doubled the support for children from economically disadvantaged families, so that they can participate in holiday and leisure activities. ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e1006	The cord shift is over.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e1011	Norway is one of the least unequal countries in the world. We also have a high standard of living, and we have high trust in each other. Many conditions have contributed to this good situation. In Norway, the differences in market revenue are smaller than in most other OECD countries. In addition, we have built up comprehensive welfare systems that provide education, health care and care to all. This helps to balance differences and to create a society with opportunities for all. If we cross the economic cycle, Norway has had relatively high economic growth in the last 30 years. This growth has benefited all groups of the population. For the 80 pst. of the population that accumulates around the middle of the distribution of income, purchasing power has increased by 75 pst. to 85 pst. during this period. Nevertheless, we also experience in Norway that income is distributed a little less equally than 30 years ago. For the low income age, the growth in purchasing power has been weaker than for the large majority, while at the top it has grown more strongly, especially in the 1990s. There are also more children living in families with persistently low incomes than ten years ago. The high level of immigration into Norway in the last 15 years is an important part of the explanation of a slightly different distribution of income. Children with a migrant background are overrepresented in the low-income group, and it is primarily the increased entry of children with a migrant background that explains the increase in low-income among children in recent years. 85 pst. The increase in child poverty is among immigrants. This development worries me. It is serious that more and more children fall into this category. We know that poverty affects health, educational opportunities and social networking. This is why the government has launched an integration promise to increase education and work, as well as a specific youth effort against jobseekers under 30 years of age. The most important thing for the government is to give all children good opportunities to succeed. As a society, we can't afford anyone falling outside already as a child because of their parents' income. In recent years, the distribution of income has also been influenced by cyclical developments. The fall in the price of oil in 2014 led to a severe setback for the Norwegian economy. From 2014 to 2016 there was a decrease in purchasing power, so to speak, all groups. This decline turned to a rise again in 2017, and the rise was strongest for the lowest decile. However, growth in income has not been strong enough to bring individual groups back to previous peak levels. In the longer term, conditions such as technological development and globalisation have also influenced the distribution of income. For example, EU enlargement in 2004 contributed to rapid growth in Norway’s population over the next 15 years, and those with a background from other countries are overrepresented in the lower parts of the distribution of income. The government works purposefully, and in a broad field, for a society with little differences and opportunities for all. But there are not only small differences that make Norway one of the world’s best countries to live in. High standards of living are also important for our quality of life. In a modern economy, income levels and distribution of income remain in an interplay between many relationships. Our high incomes are generated through efforts by employees and businesses. They are based on labour input and high productivity. Efforts at education and will and ability to change also mean something. Effective use of our total resources is crucial for the level of income, and therefore for how much can be shared. That is why we need to protect the basic conditions for economic growth and stability, including in a distribution perspective. We know that taxes and transfers mean a lot to the distribution of income and purchasing power in Norway. Taxes and transfers contribute to the reduction of the inequality between households in Norway by the order of 40 pst. The contribution has been quite stable over time. The differences between people are reduced by another 20 pst. if we also take into account the value of important public services. Researchers in the SSB have calculated that when we take into account the value of services such as kindergarten, education, health and care, Norway comes out with the least inequality of the countries in Europe covered by the survey. The tax system is the main source of funding for our comprehensive welfare systems, and yes: the tax system is redistributive. However, we must also take seriously that taxes and transfers affect labour supply, saving, risk-taking and investment. As a result, taxes and transfers also affect the ability to convert and grow. Both taxes and transfers need to be designed with care, so that they motivate efforts and facilitate the good use of our overall resources. Policies to limit inequality and strengthen opportunities for all should not be reduced to a question of taxes and transfers. One of the most important things we can do to combat inequality is to get more people in work, better integration and more people's skills through education. This has led the government's policy. We are taking the lead in inclusion work, so that the positive developments in the labour market can be used to get more of those outside the labour market to work. The government’s integration strategy paved the way for reducing exclusion and increasing employment among immigrants. Early efforts, intensive training, the strengthening of the professions and the adaptation of higher education to the needs of the labour market and the desire of the students strengthen our skills and reduce our absence. Completed secondary education provides an important basis for successful entry into and subsequent careers in the labour market. The rate of continuation within five years has been stable between 67 pst. and 71 pst. since measurements started 17 years ago. The government has implemented several measures to prevent non-response and the functioning of these measures. Students learn more and are more present, absenteeism declines, fewer falls and the proportion of those completing secondary education increases. The government will continue to pursue a policy of preserving a society of small differences, where wealth growth benefits everyone and where there is security of well-being. Small differences and opportunities for all build communities and trust within the Norwegian community. Our living conditions and distribution policies range widely from macroeconomic stability, active competition and the efficient use of society's resources, through trust building and interaction to solve common tasks, to education, integration, inclusion and work for each of us.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e1205	A week ago, Eva Britt Søvdsnes Myklebust visited my office. She has a long career behind her in fishing and since in care. She had problems with her health and moved her work capacity to an area where she mastered the situation. She wanted to work, she wanted to contribute. She has paid taxes and stood by the community. So, while she was waiting to be clarified, the money stopped, because the government decided to cut into this social welfare scheme. Eva Britt's story tells of one of the many ways into inequality, about inequality we could have restricted, stopped and turned around with politics, and about the consequences of the majority's policy in Norway, where we can afford to act as a community, while apparently we can't afford to give those who really need it, the help they deserve – so we can afford to give those who have the most relief and tax relief, but not those who really need help to get on with it. We have a government that without modesty leads to reduced expenditure on redundancy payments, because they have cut deep into the scheme. This is one of many ways of inequality. Differences between people are increasing. We agree on that. It is documented in black and white in the message we are discussing, which the government was required by Stortinget to produce. It shows that income inequality has increased during this government. The wealth inequality has grown dramatically under this government. Measured with wealth, we have a higher inequality than other OECD countries, such as the United States and Japan. United Kingdom and France. Few would have thought so. The richest 10 percent in Norway now own 60 pst. of the wealth of the country. Three out of ten households have reduced their purchasing power between 2013 and 2017, while the richest have become richer. Those with the most have got 8.1 billion. kr in tax cuts. In this message, we see that the government does not take an overall approach to limit the many ways of inequality. The launch of the message also shows that they are double wrong about the increased differences, including: fault of reason. Before the notification was made, the Minister of Finance said that the main cause of increasing inequality is labour immigration or immigration at large. Error, the SSB said a few hours later. And the government is wrong about the medicine against the differences. No simple action provides us with the solution to this challenge. Differences that grow are demanding to turn around. For this reason, it is so vital that the policy as a whole counteracts growing unemployment and inequality, that it gives all people the opportunity to enjoy good health, education and training, work to live off, the opportunity for everyone to live a safe old age. The government's message lacks a comprehensive policy to outsource such a course. This is why the Workers' Party, working on an alternative story, has made an alternative communication of inequality concrete by 83 actions across a number of policy areas — from work to transport to health, which will reduce differences in Norway. Just a few examples: school food for all primary school children, free SFOs for first-graders – regardless of the economy, pension from the first crown – one million Norwegians don't have it, regulating the pension so that no pensioners get less purchasing power when employees get more purchasing power, more money to the Husbank, so that they are able to ensure that people get housing, and almost 80 other measures. Even with such a policy, the inequalities would not go down from one year to another. But that would make a difference. And more fundamentally, combating increased differences is about what society we want. Here I believe there is one of the most obvious differences in course and direction between a Right and Advance Party-led government and a red-green alternative. A universal welfare state that offers a good and equivalent offer to everyone is a carrier of Norwegian society. A core value is that it actively contributes to opportunities for all and counteracts inequality. We have traditionally had high trust and close association in Norway. Increased differences threaten all of this. It also weakens our ability to face changes — ranging from cutting climate emissions and major changes in the Norwegian economy to the use of new technologies. The OECD and the IMF call inequality poison for conversion. In fact, crime in terms of profit and violence does not increase. For most people, this is a safe society. But it's dark clouds. From international studies, we know that increasing inequality in countries has major adverse effects. In such countries, trust among people has become worse. There will be more violence, more violence, more crime, and more people in prison. Why is that? Scientists say that when inequalities increase and people see others drift away, run away with good things and take advantage of the community, they experience themselves as losers in the inequality struggle. This leads to increased risk behaviour — in total from gambling, irresponsible borrowing, risky health, more crime and violence. So this is not about poverty. It's about differences. Fortunately, despite increasing inequality in Norway, this has not yet taken place — so far. But can we be sure that this will continue? If we look at other countries, the answer is no. If we are to secure this trust-based society, prevent the blossoming of crime, juvenile delinquency, recriminalism, attitudes like it's okay to break in, etc., then we also need to ensure for the future that the development of increased economic inequality does not continue. It is not certain that in ten years I will have the same good experience on the ferry that I had recently. If this government and the next government are not able to reverse this, governments will also have to assume responsibility for the fact that crime in Norway will increase. It's a serious responsibility to take, and then it doesn't help to try to prevent crime with increased penalties, double penalties on Holmlia, etc., when you've put yourself up to structural conditions that make crime increase.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e2071	Agenda 2030 and sustainability targets are the road map for government work. The targets are only ten years away, and if they are to be achieved, efforts need to be intensified in a number of areas. Let me summarize three of the main challenges. Mobilising national resources for development will be crucial. 70 pst. the cost of achieving sustainability targets must be borne by developing countries themselves. Key elements of government’s work are fiscal efforts, the fight against illegal capital flows and corruption and the development of a productive and competitive private sector. Our work on national resource mobilisation is also highlighted by the OECD DAC in their assessment of Norwegian aid, which was presented earlier today and mentioned by several of the representatives. Here I would also like to stress that it will not be possible to achieve the sustainability objectives without functioning countries and institutions in developing countries. Responsibility for ownership and management of developments must lie with national authorities, and a main objective of development cooperation is to strengthen this precisely and to contribute to their long-term independence from assistance. Therefore, national capacity and state institutions in the countries themselves are so important. It must apply to Norwegian support through multilateral channels as well as to Norwegian bilateral cooperation. We want to work more on this, and that's why we also make it clear in the partner country. The second challenge is climate change, which threatens to hit the ground under much of the work done in relation to development. The government will continue its efforts to reduce expenditure, but in addition we will make climate adaptation, hunger reduction and prevention of natural disasters and crises a major area of Norwegian aid. By doing so, we may be helping to respond to the poor country most of all: how to adapt to the climate change that has already taken place. Here it is to contribute to a climate-smart and more productive agriculture entirely central. The third main challenge is about the fundamental principle throughout the 2030 agenda: that no one should be left out – «leave no one behinderd». The least developed countries have received an increasing share of world aid in recent years. The government is going the opposite way and will help reverse the trend — more will go to those who need it the most. Through new programmes focused on combating modern slavery, strengthening the efforts of people with disabilities and combating harmful customs, we place work on vulnerable and marginalised groups at the centre of Norwegian development policy. Again, no one should be left out. The work for sexual and reproductive health and rights is literally vital, as it is said here. It embraces a wide range of rights, such as access to sexual education and contraception, safe pregnancy and childbirth, the prevention and treatment of sexually transmitted diseases, and family planning. These are measures that all help to avoid dangerous abortions for both mother and child, and that are life-saving. The government takes a sharp distance from, and strongly disagrees with, the line the US administration has put on with the global gag rule, and we have recently expressed our concern in two written recordings to the U.S. Department of Foreign Affairs. This situation is reinforced by the fact that the SRHR is being weakened in other countries as well as in international forums. It can lead to the loss of more lives, not the opposite. In addition, Trump’s policies contribute to the weakening of the work referred to here – related to both HIV and AIDS, prevention, the fight against malaria and the fight against unsafe and dangerous abortions. That is deeply worrying. It is important that Norway defends and strengthens this. So we're going to give about 1.3 billion. kr for that work. Norway is now the largest contributor to the UN Population Fund, and we will continue to work to strengthen the SRHR internationally. I want to thank you for the good input that has come, and it's exciting that it happens on a day when the OECD DAC is presenting its report. We're going to work everything we can to ensure an even more efficient use of resources, so that the money goes even further for those who need it most.

If you compare Norway with other countries, you can see that Troms and Finnmark, as I was in the city – the coercive Troms and Finnmark – are larger in area than Ireland. So, of course, it's more demanding to do emergency work in Norway than it is in many other European countries. We basically have a good emergency response in Norway, but we had an emergency selection, and we have processes where we have concluded that we have to do things even better. Unfortunately, in its preparedness work, the government has had one conclusion all along, and it is that to do things better, centralisation is the answer – and it is to make it so-called more robust. We've seen it especially in the police sector, where the number of places of employment has been reduced by over 120. There has been a great deal of local concern, and there is also a great deal of internal concern in the police that emergency services have suffered, and not least prevention. But it's been the answer to the government: centralization. When you go into the most basic emergency units: ambulances, health, police and fire, which are municipal, it's interesting to see: who's going first? Who is it that comes first to the place when things happen? It's basically the fire service that's only at the point of damage when all three units are called out simultaneously, and the fire service is the local, the close, maybe what the government would say is the least robust, because it's not centralized, but it's close to where people live. In addition, it is prescribed that they have a response time in the fire service, as opposed to what we have in the ambulance service. Let's go a little bit into the numbers when it comes to the country's emergency services. What's going on? Take Finnmark as an example. In 2016, the fire service was first at the point of damage in 52.7 p.m. of the cases. So two years went by, and the government is implementing its reforms, its actions. Then it has become so that the fire service is first at the point of damage in 60,2 p.m. of the cases. So the state, whether it's an ambulance or a police force, rarely comes first. It is the local fire service that does — the local, often part-time fireman — that has to go into the situation. If you look at it nationally, you can see that the health service, for which Councillor Bent Höie is responsible, was first at the place of injury in 29.9 p.m. of the cases in 2016. It fell to 27 pst. in 2018. The police were first at the point of damage in 17,5 p.m. of the cases in 2016 – it fell to 15.3 pst. in 2018. The fire department, the room, the nearby, was before under 50 pst, in 2018 it was at 54.8 pst. So this is in two years. The figures from the Directorate for Social Security and Preparedness show one thing, that the state is becoming involved in much of the close preparedness work around Norway. What we have to discuss here in Stortinget is: do we think it's right, or do we think it's wrong? And then you might have to be open about that, those who think it's right that more and more of the responsibility should be transferred to the local part-time fireman. We in the Center Party believe it is wrong and have been clear about it over time. Last week the Minister of Emergency was here in Stortinget. She took no responsibility for anything, shifting responsibility to everyone else. Now the Minister of Health is here, and he is at least responsible for ambulances. The response from the Minister of Emergency Response on behalf of the government last week, when we noted that it was a challenge that the fire service came more and more often first, was: I am ‘ glad that someone is coming’. That's a statement I don't think the government can support, but that's what was said last week by the Minister of Emergency Response. So to the discussion that I hope Bent Höie can go right into. Today, we have indicative response times within the Ambulance Service of 12 minutes in central strokes and 25 minutes in so-called pig-grown strokes, and in none of the country's counties, this target has been achieved. It's a fact, we're not near that goal in any of the country's counties. Then is my first challenge to the Council of State: Does the Council of State think it's okay that we're not near that goal? If he thinks it's okay, he gets to be honest and open on it, that he thinks it's okay that it's the fire service that more and more should act first. And if he doesn't think it's okay, what action will the Council of State take, when the Right to Now, at least – and the Government – has been against legalising response time? And then challenge number. 2: Doesn't the Council of State also see, when you have the processes we see now in Trøndelag, parts of Nord-Norway and Møre and Romesdal, that a law of response time will give a very clear lead to the health business of the country that you can't carry out the centralisation projects that are currently at the discussion stage with regard to the country's ambulance service, that it will be a very strong political leadership, and that it can be an effective tool, just as it has been for the fire service? I hope that even if the government parties almost do not set up at all – even none of the 92 had the opportunity to set up the radio this morning early – Bent Höie will be able to give a good answer on how we can get close to the response time. That's point number. One, because that's the goal. And why can't he possibly be open that a law can be a good way to force health businesses to prioritize the close ambulance service more than what we see today? We cannot live with the fact that more and more of the local emergency work is going to go from health and police to the local fire department.

They challenge the editor - controlled press and trust in it, and unfortunately they get hearing in ever - growing circles. Since our democracy is totally dependent on the people having great confidence in the media in order to have an informed and good debate, we are struggling when it comes to rocking the truths we are to be enlightened by. When the population more often and more often experience or get told that news they believe is false, or that campaign journalism disguised as objective news from alternative media appears credible, while the same alternative media claim that editor-directed media do not write the truth – yes, it gets confusing. Suddenly, we can get to a point where the population really doesn't have enough confidence in the media anymore and we're in a situation where we don't have a common point of view for what's true and real of information. How, then, is the policy, the debate, and our democracy going? What informed debate do we get when what we are discussing is not based on a common understanding of the facts? This worries me. And that worries many others. This challenge is addressed in the media communication addressed in the Committee, but there may not be so many specific solutions addressed. That's why I've traveled this interpellation today, where I point out how important it is for the public to have confidence in media journalism to maintain our democracy, and where I ask our media minister how she will help to strengthen confidence in our editor-driven journalism. It is my clear opinion that the system that editor-directed media have worked after, with the Vær Varsom poster, with the Editor's poster and with the Press Professional Committee as a complaint body, has worked very well for many decades. It has provided journalists with ethical guidance to present society-friendly, informative, important and true news to the population – which I think they have basically really succeeded in. The trust in Norwegian media has also been very high. But now it's descending, showed the media survey, which was presented during the Media Days in May. It was found that confidence in the media has fallen from 85 p.m. to 81 pst. in one year. Now this is probably due to more things than the emergence of fake news and alternative media, but the media survey also told us that the whole of 42 pst. The Norwegian media often give an incorrect picture of the Norwegian community, in whole or in part, agreed. And 47 pst. this year versus 41 pst. Last year, the Internet and social media have made traditional media less important for democracy. These aren't very good numbers, I think. So what can we do? I've asked a lot of people in and out of the media industry about the last year. Most people feel discouraged. Some talk about shutting down pages and shutting down social media, others about denying different types of content and turning down much harder on publishing and spreading false news. I believe that shutting down and refusing websites in the name of freedom of expression is not the way to go. So what else can we do? Well, of course, we can work so that the population gets more knowledge about how to reveal ‘fake news’, understand that they are exposed to campaign journalism, or that it is advertising content and not an objective journalistic article they are reading. There is a need, not least in the older garden, for yesterday’s launch of a study by the Media Authority. Half of those over 60 years of age are unable to reveal false news or are unsure whether news is fake, and even those under 30 years of age are struggling. The media surveillance says we need a power cap to increase critical media awareness. Yes, well, I say -- a little uncertain about how, but I know that we've got a teaching system to learn more about revealing 'fake news' to young people between 13 and 18 years of age in school, which I hope works well, from which you might learn, and which can be extended to other groups. Also NRK Super has recently had a greater interest in learning children about critical thinking and fake news, among other things. through the drama series ‘The Hunt for Truth’. I welcome this and think that the NRK should follow up for other groups as well. Both they and other serious media should not be afraid to appear unnecessarily politically correct or self-absorbed if they in the future make more substance on how their own journalism is actually designed according to strict ethical guidelines. But then we have new challenges that come, like "deep fakes," so very advanced manipulation of moving images. This is a technology that Reuters says during the media days that they're trying to reveal. But it's very difficult, because those who want to reveal this kind of "fakes" will always lie a horse's head behind. Initiative as indeed.no I am also pleased. I think that's a matter of fact checking by day. But those kind of fact checkers probably don't care a lot about some alternative media. They just want to say that fact checks are not facts. So in my opinion, it boils down to the credibility of the sender. Is the sender of a film clip or a case credible as a result of the fact that as a reader, listener or titter I know that the sender is serious, editor-in-chief and has a good reputation because they have repeatedly shown that they conduct good and verifiable journalism, then they have great opportunities to maintain my trust. One of the best ways to maintain confidence in editorial media is according to the industry itself, both in hearings and when I have asked them, that they have the opportunity to make really good journalism. The more good disclosures, references, descriptions, reports and stories in the media that set the agenda, the better the situation of people, and the better the policy and political decision-making process, the stronger the people's desire and confidence in the media. So as politicians, how can we help to get more good, thorough and verifiable journalism at a time the media itself describes as critical because they are struggling with ad failure because of Google and Facebook, demanding digitisation and conversion phases, and not least the emergence of ‘fake news’ and alternative media? We in the Family and Culture Committee were in Washington in September and visited Newseum and Freedom Forum. That's where we met the boss, the old newsman Gene Policinski, who is an avid social debater and one of the founders of the United States Today. We believe that, as politicians, we have a responsibility to help strengthen confidence in this journalism and in the sender, and we hope that the Minister for Media, who has the highest responsibility for media policy in the country, agrees – and that she has some good suggestions for how.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e2542	The editorial media of journalistic mass media have traditionally been considered a particularly important element of our democratic infrastructure. These media play a central role in the political debate and in the formation of opinions – which is at the heart of our democratic justification for freedom of expression. The development of new media forms has fundamentally changed the framework conditions for traditional mass media. With a number of new opportunities for communication, everyone can be a publisher today. At the same time, we know that the information we have is not always credible or has the trust or quality on which the informed democratic conversation and the formation of opinion should be based. One of the challenges facing current media use is to distinguish good information from poor, well-founded views from conspiracy theories and balanced representations from pre-conceived opinions. The editor and editor-driven journalistic media have a crucial role to play here. We don't just need a variety of media and journalism. Trust is also necessary. When we make an opinion about politics or other societal issues, the information and perspectives we receive through the media are a central part of our basis of opinion. If we can't trust that the selection and presentation of media issues are relevant, truthful and based on expertise, we lose an important basis for opinion formation and discussion. That we trust that the media are sources of credible information is therefore a prerequisite for the media to carry out its democratic task. If the media are to have a democratic function, they must be independent of the power structures they are to monitor. In addition, the public must have confidence in that independence. Only then can media content provide a basis for free and informed political opinion. This is why editorial independence from authorities is both a political objective in itself and a concern behind the design of the support schemes, legislation and management systems that we have in the media field. Today, we have good measures through media support, both indirect support in the form of VAT exemptions for news media and direct media support. Here the government has already taken action to strengthen Norwegian media. Electronic news media have been exempted from VAT and production grants have been made platform neutral. There have been important measures to improve the conditions for the news media to produce good and thorough journalism. Another measure taken by the government is the establishment of a grant scheme for innovation and development. The aim of this mechanism is to stimulate editorial, content-oriented innovation and to develop news and timeliness media. We see the need for such a system. So we've increased the pot by 3 million. NOK, up to 10 million. kr. In addition, we have an agreement with TV 2. In total, this year we have increased the direct media support by 149.5 million. kr. It is the largest increase in direct media support since the scheme was introduced 50 years ago. Technological and economic developments in the industry have created a growing need for innovation. When disinformation is disseminated through social media, the direct digital presence of traditional media becomes even more important. At the same time, we know that the digital transformation requires financial resources. Therefore, it is gratifying that 98 pst. of the year’s innovation grants have gone to small, local media or to the innovation of local content. Now we want to take action to ensure that we maintain the network we have of local guides, and help to ensure that we have local guides across the country with enough resources to carry out their community mission. In 2018, BI conducted a study on the digitisation of local media use and confidence in different local media. Here, 67 pst. that they had high or very high confidence in their locality. Very few, only 6 pst., replied that they had low or very low confidence in their locality, in NRK district missions and in regional newspapers. These numbers tell us that we have high confidence in the media that is the densest of our everyday lives. However, a local view is not enough in itself. Local newspapers must also have financial and editorial resources to carry out their social mission. We know that there are differences from newspaper to newspaper in the ability to prioritize resource-intensive journalism, whether it is critical and investigative journalism, journalism that explains to us events and subject areas that may be difficult to understand, or the issues that bring new perspectives that are important for public vocabulary. The government wants to bet more on the smaller locals. In 2018, 158 newspapers received press support, but ten of these stacks of farms with two thirds of the pot. I think that challenges the legitimacy of the support system we have. So we're going to make a redistribution into small local newspapers. At the same time, in a transitional phase, we will increase the total appropriations, so that the media that in the future will have to get a little less, give time and opportunity to change. We will also increase predictability by setting the amount of media support for four years at a time. In addition to continuing to work for the continued diversity of media – thematic and geographical – we will work to preserve the media diversity that people trust. The government wants the media to be as independent as possible from political authorities. The media public is a maritime building block in society that should be able to develop on its own premises, without government control. We strengthen the arm's length principle, and I believe that we also strengthen confidence in the media that receive support as independent media. That's why we want to set up a media support council. With such a mechanism, we will strengthen the legitimacy of the grant system and build on the trust in the system and in the media receiving grants. This does not mean, however, that the Media Support Council should have full powers of attorney. The regulatory framework for grant schemes will ensure transparency of the Council's activities and changes to the legislation will have to be sent to consultation, as at present. Last year the government sent out a proposal for a new Media Liability Act. The legislative proposal provides for an update, clarification and consolidation of specific rules on legal liability for the media area and also introduces some new legal provisions. With the new Media Liability Act, the government wants, among other things, facilitating the serious management and control of media, based on media ethics and principles. I believe that the law that supports the editor-driven journalistic media is an important element in fulfilling the state's infrastructure responsibility. Strong and independent media are the best medicine we have against disinformation and hateful statements. Therefore, we should continue to have a media policy that facilitates freedom of expression, freedom of the press and freedom of information, a policy that facilitates good news production across the country, and a wide-ranging public conversation in the digital society.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e2742	It's been many years since there's been a big discussion about the annual review of Norway's fisheries agreements, and I'm not going to make it a big discussion today either. The SV and the Central Party have raised a fundamental discussion in the setting up of the exchange relationship issue linked to the third country's quota, with the EU in particular. I just have to explain it to the assembly. From the target band to the fishing quota, the International Council for the Exploration of the Sea comes first with its recommendation on the tolerance of the different species of fish, and nations negotiate the allocation of the total quota to the countries on the basis of that Council. Then, at a later stage, this is distributed among the various groups of vessels. But before this happens, before a benefit to the groups of vessels, an agreement is reached with the EU on the exchange of fish. In short, it has been Norwegian practice to give up cod, which coastal fishermen can fish, and to replace species that can benefit only parts of the sea-going fleet. Why is it problematic? Yes, because we have rungs in this country for how, among other things. the cod shall be distributed, the so-called trawl trail, which, in short, implies about 30 pst. shall be taken from the seagoing fleet and the rest, 70 pst., shall be taken from the coastal fleet. When the fish are taken before distribution, that means that an actual rocker is at the root of Norwegian fishing. It is problematic when such a large part of the cod is given to the EU, and the only ones that can benefit from it are the ocean-going fleet. It's problematic, and what I think undermines the Norwegian system we've built up over time. In principle, we believe that if we are to have a proper understanding of what the beam path covers, it must of course cover the entire Norwegian total quota on cod. One can't take anything first, distribute, and then just stick to what's left to distribute as part of this. I see that some in the setting are trying to make a point that this criticism is rocking the EU's contractual relationship. It has nothing to do with it. We recognise that we are in a trading relationship with the European Union. We've been doing that for a long time, and there are long historical reasons for it. That's how one organizes this exchange relationship on the Norwegian side we think is contrary to the basis for the distribution between the fleet groups.

The members of the Committee of the Centre Party and the Socialist Left believe that when exchanging fishing quotas, we should take into account that ‘the fish to be exchanged can be fished as much as possible by the same groups of vessels as the fish to be exchanged’. The system of quota exchange through negotiations with other countries dates from the 1970s, when coastal states established their economic zones. Since then, Norway has been aiming to protect its interests in the areas of quota-exchange negotiations in other countries. It's not like we're swapping someone's quotas, but we have a quota exchange to maintain our traditional fisheries along the coasts of other countries. Fishing on the quotas we receive in other zones involves long-distance transport under some severe weather conditions, and it is difficult to imagine smaller vessels participating in such fisheries. Unutilised third country quotas go back to the Norwegian available quota in the fisheries concerned.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e3106	It's blown up to storm in the wind power debate in the last few months, and there's strong feelings on all sides. Some of us have been in the storm, but more of us need enough to breathe a little bit more with our stomachs when we talk about wind power. A few weeks ago, the Committee on Energy and the Environment was travelling to learn more about how we can go even further in the field of climate – how we can build green and profitable industries, and how we can learn from what they do in other countries. We came home with a lot of useful knowledge, but the most important thing – and we're all going to take it with us, regardless of party – is that Norway is already the world's best country in terms of climate. And it was just supposed to be missing. With the story we have in our back pocket about fossil energy, I think we sometimes forget that we need to do an extra amount in the climate field. We're already doing a great job, and we can be proud of what we've gotten together so far. But we're far from the goal. We have to go further, and we have to be able to have more thoughts in our head at the same time. Because a climate policy that takes us back to the past is no use – yes, almost all the way back to when the fossils lived in the best of order. We need a modern and sustainable climate policy, which also ensures something to live on in the future. Norway has opted for oil. We live in one of the richest countries in the world, among others. Because we've been surfing the fossil wave. That is why we have an extra responsibility as we face the climate struggle of the times. The Earth is a mutilation, and our children march in the streets to make us in this house wake up. We see the changes here in Norway: it is warmer, wetter and wilder weather, and there are fewer skis this year because the snow disappears. There has been more rain, storm and drought, and the sewer pipes, which have had the right size for 100 years, now flood in Norway, and bacteria flow into several waters. It makes people sick, and that means that our tax money has to be used to replace today's pipes and to build better sewage systems, so the water still has to be drinkable. Climate change is here, and we have to act, including: by expanding renewable energy – water, wind and sun. Now that wind power has been blowing on land, many of us have wanted more politicians far at sea. Of course, we listen to that. Norway has been working with sea wind for quite a long time, but the time has come to step up the effort and insert an extra gear, and here the government has to sit in the driver’s seat. As the world's best offshore, Norway has an enormous potential for bet on sea winds, but we have to keep our tongues straight in the mouth. Our beautiful, long-haul coast is not very long-haul, which means that there is no bottom-water wind we should bet on. Floating sea winds, on the other hand, we have a comparative advantage to operate with. Bottom sea winds are logically limited enough by depth and, if the installation is liquid, a larger market for sea wind is opened. By going further at sea you can also build in areas with even more wind, and we get even more power out of each installed megawatt. It offers the opportunity to cut emissions, while at the same time ensuring Norwegian offshore supply industry in the future. Norway has strong competence and tradition for maritime offshore value chains that can be easily transferred to renewable offshore industries. Simply put, we have a pretty good idea of making money on floating dolls along our coast. Marine wind will cut emissions, create jobs, and it has the potential to be a value-creating technology export. Because this is primarily about – the export of technology we are developing, just as other offshore and subsea industries are already doing. We can do this in Norway. Marine wind investment must bring forward new technologies and more than one supplier. The initiative needs to stimulate competition in order to reduce costs, and cost reduction requires sufficient volume, such as: at Equinor's Hywind Tampen. I'm very glad that the blue-green government is betting on floating sea winds, but I'm impatient. This is not going fast enough, we have to put in place the proposal for the way in which concessions are to be granted, and the areas in question, before the summer. It's great that there's already a chance for smaller demo parks, but we have to think bigger. This has the potential to become one of Norway’s largest exports. Norway has a unique opportunity to take a leading role internationally if we focus on tomorrow’s solutions today. Many countries, including Norway, as we have seen recently, have land conflicts related to wind power on land. Liquid sea winds can be a good way for several countries to secure energy supply and reach their climate commitments. This is a win-win for both Norway and the world. This means that the regulations for opening and granting concessions must be in place. This means that Enova must bet, and that means Norwep and Innovation Norway must be on the ball. That means we have to have more demo parks, and that means we have to facilitate stronger clusters. For sea wind today is expensive, very expensive. That's why it's so crucial to bring down costs and profitability. We must have good incentives on the research side, and we must have good marketing backing. Should sea wind become the adventure we dream of, we must be clear that this is something we want, not just because it is a little uncomfortable with debates about wind power on land, but because we actually want to continue to be a leading nation on offshore installations, installations that in future will produce green energy rather than fossil energy. The hope is light green, the sea is blue, it is obvious that the Lord wants the blue-green government to bet on sea wind.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e3134	The representative of Westgaard-Halle has asked the following questions: Will the government stimulate investment in floating sea winds, among others. by establishing a regulation for opening and awarding sea wind concessions, facilitating technological development through sea wind demo parks and contributing to cost reductions? I believe I can answer yes to all these questions. But first of all, I share the enthusiasm of Westgaard-Halle for wind power at sea. This is exciting technology that offers great opportunities for Norway, such as both energy nation, technology nation and not least porting. At sea wind power is also in strong growth internationally, and there are many projects in other countries where Norwegian actors can compete to deliver goods and services. In other words, we have a solid starting point in view of the experience of our renewable energy, petroleum and shipping. Marine wind power already accounts for the largest export of goods and services in the renewable sector, and Norwegian companies are at the forefront in several areas, particularly in the area of liquid wind power. The government has a broad instrument that contributes to technology development and cost reductions. Let me mention a few examples: Sea wind is a field of energy21, our national strategy for research and commercialisation of climate-friendly energy technologies. For technology development in offshore wind power, Enova is the main instrument, and for support for internationalisation, both Norway’s Energy Partners and Innovation Norway are important instruments. In the United Kingdom, Equinor has built the world’s first floating wind farm, Hywind Scotland, based on technology developed and tested in Norway, with the support of Enova. Equinor has also applied for Enova support for Hywind Tampen, which, by means of liquid wind turbines, plans to supply the petroleum activity on a Norwegian renewable cap. It is also uplifting that Unitech has taken over Equinor's Hywind turbine at Karmøy to continue the work on testing and developing liquid wind power. I am interested in the activity around Karmøy and in the technology environments in the West, and there are plans for testing new liquid wind turbines in the area. The knowledge environment around these test projects at Karmøy is also supported by the Innovation Norway Cluster Programme. I believe that the breadth and extent of the activity that takes place with support from various public schemes shows that Norwegian environments are active in offshore wind power. It also shows, in my opinion, that the instruments work well and stimulate efforts, and also contribute to cost reductions, as the representative requested in his statement. The representative also asks whether the government intends to open up areas for renewable energy at sea. We'll do that. The ministry is now in a final phase of its work, and I expect a proposal for the opening of areas and a draft regulation under the Marine Energy Act to be sent to the hearing before the summer. The Sea Energy Act requires areas to be opened by the King before a concession can be applied for for at sea for renewable energy projects, but it is already possible to apply for a concession for smaller demonstration projects and projects related to the petroleum activity. The opening of areas will allow for commercial applications for the development of offshore wind power. In order to increase predictability for actors, the concession process will be clarified by a regulation of marine energy legislation. Wind power at sea is, as I said, exciting. But as elsewhere in energy policy, it is not here that this is without conflict or without cost. The government wants to promote the development of profitable renewable energy, and I stress that we have no plans to introduce new support schemes. I also think it's a bit until we see profitability for commercial projects in offshore wind power in Norway. But the costs are falling, and the wind resources off our coast are very good. In the long term, this can become important for Norway, and I believe that a comprehensive and long-term management of marine wind resources is important. I think we will also see resistance to offshore wind projects. We're a port, there's a lot of activity in our marine areas that can be influenced by wind turbines, and there's reason to remember that there are also environmental values at sea. Nevertheless, I believe that offshore wind power has a great potential. The ambition must be that Norwegian industry will continue to be at the forefront of this market and, of course, also increase its market share, not least internationally. Here I believe we must have an ambition that the marine wind market should not only be Norway, but the whole world. At the same time, of course, we should continue to accommodate here at home and follow developments closely.

ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e3296	In the latest edition of the annual Health Policy Barometer from Kantar, 62 pst. that we have a two-part healthcare system, with a personal economic impact on how well you are being treated. 78 pst. of the asked people think this is negative, and 64 pst. believes that it helps to create greater differences between people. In recent years, the media have given us more single stories about patients with deadly diseases who have paid off their pocket to receive treatment that the public does not provide. This is worrying, especially when we know that there are basically major social differences in the health status of the population. The Public Health Report 2018 from the Institute of Public Health shows that those with the highest education live up to six years longer than they did. The social differences in life expectancy are increasing, and they are larger in Norway than in many other European countries. A report from the Statistical Central Bureau published in 2017 showed that among people over 45 years of age, almost 50 pst. of those with the lowest income that they do not have good health. Among those with the highest income, the number is only 20 pst. Similar differences are seen between levels of education. On the other hand, when we look at how many people in the different income groups actually use health services, the differences are not the same. This suggests that there are different conditions to the real need for treatment that determine the extent to which our healthcare services are poor and rich. When it comes to high-paying health services, such as medical specialists and dentists, the SSB’s figures show that high-skilled people use these services more than low-paid and low-skilled. The most important thing we can do politically to counteract a two-part healthcare system is to ensure that the public health service is so good that it is not necessary to pay for commercial services. A report from the analysis agency Menon published in 2017 asked the question: Are we spending too much money on health? Their conclusions were clear: the health sector's revenues have not been particularly high in the last ten years, and we do not use more than other countries with a corresponding high level of income. The report concludes that investment in the health sector has been downgraded in the last ten years. Although investment has increased, investment still represents less of the total cost of the specialist services. The Minister of Health promised to strengthen patient care in hospitals by 12 billion. kr in the previous grossing-up period. There was an error. However, through the reform of free treatment options, the government spends hundreds of millions of dollars a year on commercial treatment sites rather than strengthening public supply. In the Granavolden platform, the government states that they will extend free treatment options to even more areas. In addition, a free user preferences will be applied to the municipalities by model from Sweden. According to the government, there should be greater freedom of choice, but experience from Sweden shows that there is no content of the services users get to choose from, only the company that will deliver the service. On the other hand, we know from Swedish experience that the users who can pay for it are offered extra services from the commercial service providers. A Swedish public statement points out that free choice may have led to greater social and geographical inequalities. This means that free choice can provide additional two-way distribution of healthcare services in Norway as well. Much of the concern for a two-part healthcare system is that patients fear that the public health service does not offer the best treatment and that one is forced to use commercial treatment facilities to get the latest and most effective treatments. In recent years, hospitals have been given financial responsibility for more and more medicines. It's often about the most expensive drugs. Last year, Lovisenberg Diakonale Hospital went out and pointed out that it was not enough to cover the actual expenses of the new medicine hospitals. The director of the hospital calls it a hidden efficiency improvement from the government, and although health services are required to give priority to new treatments to them, there is reason to fear that underfunding of hospitals helps patients to go to private places to access the newest and best treatment methods. In winter, decision-making forums for new methods set out to say no to the introduction of combination therapy with cytotoxicity and immunotherapy against lung cancer in the Norwegian healthcare service, a treatment that was used by around 700 Norwegian patients each year. After new rounds of negotiations with great public attention, the decision-making forum turned around. It was a very good news for the Norwegian lung cancer patients, but given the great consequences it has of getting a drug yes or no, it is worrying, as the Center Party sees it, that such decisions are made behind closed doors. Since the Minister of Health has given in to the demands of the pharmaceutical industry to keep the price of medicine secret, we do not know how much health benefit per cent must be paid before the decision-making forum says yes to offering a new treatment. We don't know if the pharmaceutical companies are telling the truth when they say they've made a more reasonable offer, nor do we know if the business directors are telling the truth when they say that the price offered is too high. We don't know what that cost is. Nor can we test decision-making forums if they say no to treatment because the treatment is not cost-effective, or if they say no because the total cost of the public health service is too high. This information is kept secret. In order to counteract a two-way street, it is crucial that the population has confidence in the priorities of health services. When there is such a big secret to the priority of health funds, there will be no open debate. It is easy to understand that trust is out of place, and it may be helpful that so many people answer that one has a two-part healthcare system in Norway today. Nor should it have been necessary to purchase private health insurance in Norway, but according to statistics from Finans Norway, the number of Norwegians with private health insurance has increased steadily in recent years. In 2013, it was approx. In 2018, the number of persons insured with treatment was increased to about 400,000. 550 000. When the Right went to elections in 2013, there were 380,000 who had health insurance. I think very well that Health Minister Höie said before the election that there would not be a market for health insurance with the introduction of free treatment options. But since we've just increased our insurance costs. Several insurance companies have stressed that they should cover non-public cancer medicines. There are several trends that contribute to a greater division of health services. We see that the social health differences in the population are increasing. We see that the demand for private health insurance is increasing. More and more expensive medicines are coming on the market. However, the decision on the medicinal product to be offered by the public health service is a secret. In this situation, the government is investing heavily in using public health money on commercial service providers rather than strengthening public supply. We see a competition policy where private providers are going to compete with the public to provide publicly funded health services. There is a superior principle in the new government platform. My question is therefore whether the Council of State believes that we have a two-part healthcare system today, if more privatisation of the public health system is a government objective, and what the government is doing to prevent the development of a two-part healthcare system.
