In the 2017 survey of municipalities, over 40 pst. by the municipalities that they have an up-to-date plan for rehabilitation and rehabilitation. From preliminary KOSTRA figures for 2018 we see a positive development, and that the number of municipalities with plans has increased to approx. 50 pst. Development and political treatment of such a plan takes some time. The Health Directorate expects the proportion to increase as several municipalities receive grants for the escalation plan. In 2018, over 200 municipalities received grants, compared with 150 in 2017. The Health Directorate summary shows that there is a decrease in the number of users with individual plans, but at the same time several users have been allocated coordinators. The Health Directorate learns that this is a topic that needs clearer leadership anchoring. This is why the directorate in its supervisors has, among other things, made it clear that, for patients and users ‘with a need for long-term and coordinated services’, municipalities must meet the requirements of the statutory coordinator role and work in line with patients’ and users’ rights, cf. individual plan and coordinator law. It is up to the municipalities to organise coordinating units as they deem appropriate. The Directorate-General for Health has made recommendations in its Adviser on organisation to ensure that entities can fulfil the important role of facilitating the coordination of services. The population reports that important development work is in progress in this area. This is also a central pilot theme for structured multidisciplinary follow-up teams. The government has increased the initial, further and continuing training of staff in health and care services by nearly 170 million. kr. Total appropriations in 2019 are approximately 360 million. kr. Both the grant of the escalation plan and the grant of competence and innovation are managed by the county councillors. The Health Directorate is in dialogue with the county council members who follow the objectives of the escalation plan and who also follow developments in this area. Although the statistical base at municipal level is deficient, we have good data on the availability of resources in the municipalities through KOSTRA. With regard to the annual development of physiotherapies and ergots, this has been very positive in recent years. From 2013 to 2018 there has been a steady increase in annual developments for both groups. In addition to the legal groups included in the law on municipal health and care services today, municipalities will have, as of 2020, an ergoterapeut and psychologist’s competence in their service provision. These are both key actors in the rehabilitation and rehabilitation work in municipalities. The escalation plan contains a number of measures followed by the government. Outpatient care for children with brain damage became part of a free treatment choice in 2017. Specific allocations have been made to all regional health establishments to establish a coherent treatment and rehabilitation course for children with acquired brain damage. The ParkinsonNet pilot project is now taking place in Stavanger and Oslo. The project shows that increased competence of both professionals and users improves the quality of treatment. An evaluation shows a broad consensus that the form of work can give users greater autonomy and that there is a lower need for hospital services and hospitalisation. The increase in outpatient services has been followed up as a mission to health regions. Feedbacks show that outpatient services are being developed in several disciplines where specialist health staff travel to municipalities to treat, follow up and guide both users, relatives and municipal services. Digital solutions are also used here. St. Olav's hospital runs a project in all regions to establish multidisciplinary diagnostic centres and learning networks for patients with long-standing pain and/or fatigue conditions with unclear cause. The project has provided a national professional boost in areas such as long-term fatigue due to unclear causes for the paediatric population with complex symptomatic and painful conditions. It's e.g. Standardised patient courses for patients with pain conditions. We have made some simplifications in the legislation to make it easier to access the logoped. Here we have also commissioned the Health Directorate to go through tariffs and legislation and propose measures that could link the logopeds closer to the municipalities. We have taken a number of measures in the physiotherapy service, which together contribute to better availability, quality and patient safety. In addition, we have introduced free rehabilitation options for patients from across the country to have more equivalent access to private rehabilitation institutions. We have started work on phased into rehabilitation services in the authorisation system for free treatment choices. Packing processes shall also be established for different patient groups. Recent consultations have been held on the package pathways for the post-brain stroke rehabilitation phase. Packages are also planned for pain, muscle and skeletal diseases and fatigue disorders. In addition, other good initiatives and projects are being undertaken by municipalities in this field. 2017 was the first year of the planning period. The experience of municipal incentive grants in other areas suggests that building capacity and competence on municipal services takes time. I therefore believe that the effect of the stimulation grant and the increase in the municipal framework on the basis of the escalation plan will only be seen after a few years. It is therefore important to follow the progress made to see if the negative trend has reversed in some areas. I will ensure that the Health Directorate is commissioned to evaluate the measures throughout the planning period, so that we can get a better basis for concluding after the end of the planning period in 2019.

After the Communist regimes in Eastern and Central Europe were forced to give up power at the end of the 1980s, the threat of the strengthening of democracy, the rule of law and human rights in this part of Europe grew. In recent years, Erdogan has developed a centralist presidential government. The regime controls the large media houses, has weakened the independence of the courts and restricted freedom of expression. He recently pushed through the cancellation of the Istanbul election result after the opposition candidate won the election. After Viktor Orbán’s failure in 2010, Hungary carried out a drastic transformation with a strong centralisation of power, not least the government’s dominance over the media, the courts and other important institutions. In the later elections he has used a rhetorical mark of anti-refugees, anti-Islam, anti-EU, anti-FN and not least anti-Soros. Why? He believes the EU is failing that it is Hungary that defends the Christian Europe against Islam and protects liberal values. But the civil society in Poland is stronger than in Hungary. Some of the attempts to take over the courts with their own support players have also been halted by the EU — an EU that has done an important job. In the last two years, the leadership role of the United States, supported by democracy, has changed and weakened, which the Foreign Minister also pointed out in his statement earlier this spring. The West no longer appears in the same way as a single block when it comes to promoting democracy. President Trump will be seen as less collusive, more divisive. That he wants to promote the interests of the United States is understandable and not strange. What is troubling is that he has reduced the contribution of the United States to international organisations, for example. by withdrawing the United States from the UN Human Rights Council. He has hailed authoritarian leaders and contributed to a new political climate of nationalism, trade war and major action policy. It may weaken the international legal order. There is reason to note how democracy in several countries is being undermined. It usually doesn't happen at the military coup. The methods used are others. The political opposition is stigmatized. Important institutions, including the judiciary and the most important mass media, are subject to direct control by the government. Civil rights are restricted, so it becomes difficult to organize or criticise the government. Power is centralized around a strong president, as we see in Azerbaijan, Russia, Turkey and Belarus, around the government, like in Hungary, or around a powerful person outside the formal apparatus of power that pulls the strings, something like this. see in Poland. There will be a peak culture of corruption in which the political and economic elite cooperate to make one another rich at the expense of the people. We must be on guard against authoritarian behaviour by political leaders in democracy. The warning lights should focus when this happens – when they reject or express weak support for democratic players, when they reject the legitimacy of political opponents, when they tolerate or encourage power, and when they try to restrict the civil rights of their opponents and of the media. We see signs of a weakening of democracy. When it comes to knowing how best to act to defend democracy and the rule of law, when democratic politicians in Europe see such tendencies in their own circles, they have to take responsibility. We have to take responsibility. Party loyalty must not cause anyone to fail to defend democracy. It's rarely going well, and some times it's going completely wrong. It's our story. Fortunately, we're not there today. I don't want to compare what's happening, neither Hitler's Nazism nor Stalin's communism. It was totalitarian regimes and ideologies. Today, our task is to defend democracy, the rule of law and human rights and to prevent the confusion of the pillars of liberal democracy. We have to defend values that we believe are decisive for our free society. We must give Norwegian and international support to democratic political forces, to civil society, to independent media and to justice. It can play an important prevention role in the longer term.
ParlaMint-NO_2019-05-28.ana.ud12e3610	The representative, Mr Hareide, addresses a very important issue — and he should not in any way have a bad conscience for the late debate, rather on the contrary — because developments in parts of Europe give cause for concern. In Hungary, the Governing Party confirmed its dominant position. In Poland and Romania, on the other hand, the opposition became more disillusioned, and it is an important correction by the electorate. A theme that has remained in these countries in recent years is an increasing pressure on the rule of law. This is a development we have every reason to be concerned about. The rule of law constitutes the backbone of the democratic system, and when the backbone is confused, the whole structure may collapse. Without an independent judiciary, the legal certainty of minorities is particularly vulnerable. Those who are not directly affected do not like to see the long-term consequences of legally elected governments strengthening their own position at the expense of legal principles and legal certainty. Developments are not only serious for the population in the countries concerned, they can also have consequences for Norway and for European cooperation Norway is part of. A safe Europe is a Europe that builds up dividing lines that give rise to conflict and instability. Developments in Poland, Hungary and Romania are of concern and the EU has launched a debate on whether the Union should have more effective instruments to ensure respect for the EU's values and rule of law. In its draft new long-term budget, the Commission has put forward proposals for a mechanism linking compliance with the rule of law to transfers from the EU budget, on the grounds that EU funds need to be adequately secured. In addition, Germany and Belgium have recently submitted a proposal for a peer review system, whereby the Member States are to monitor through the Council the development of democracy and the rule of law in all EU Member States. Norway supports the EU's efforts to promote respect for fundamental rights, the rule of law and democracy, and we closely follow the EU's processes towards Poland and Hungary. We have repeatedly encouraged countries to cooperate with the EU institutions, and we note that in December 2018 Poland chose to amend the new law on Supreme Court under the orders of the European Court of Justice. Regional actors to whom the Council of Europe and the OSCE have appropriate tools to attract. The Venice Commission's advisory opinions are a good example. The EU's various tools, primarily Article 7, on breaches of fundamental values, are also an option the EU uses, but of course it has its limitations. The challenge for all is that they require real commitment to compliance with the country’s own leaders. Current instruments for Norway are the presence and dialogue, targeted support for civil society actors, independent media and judicial reform in line with fundamental principles of the rule of law. The UN's periodical land hearings, UPR, have proved to be an effective starting point for dialogue with authorities. It is important that we communicate why it is in the country's own interest to protect democracy, the rule of law and respect for human rights. Through the EEA funds, Norway contributes to social and economic cohesion in 15 European countries. The funds contribute, inter alia, to: to strengthen civil society and the justice sector in the recipient countries, in line with the principles of rule of law, democracy and good governance. Interpellant asks whether government will use EEA funds even more specifically to promote democracy and a stronger civil society. The main profile of EEA funds in the current period, i.e. 2014-2021, is defined in agreement between donor countries and the EU, followed by national negotiations with the beneficiary countries. Agreements have been concluded with 14 of the 15 beneficiary countries. The funds for the justice sector and judicial reform have been significantly strengthened in relation to previous periods. In addition, the reserve for the civil society fund has increased and now represents at least 10 pst. of the total transfers, and the requirement for independent management of the fund, i.e. independent of the national authorities, has been strengthened in relation to the previous period. We do this in order to seek to contribute to a stronger civil society, which is an important part of any well-functioning democracy. We are still negotiating with Hungary, where it remains to be agreed first and foremost on the management of the Civil Society Fund. The donor countries, Iceland, Liechtenstein and Norway, are united on our position in the negotiations. Our approach is also supported by the Commission and by key EU countries. This is a problem I have kept the European Commission constantly up-to-date. I have also discussed this in my presentations in Stortinget, and I feel that there is cross-political support for the government's line. We hope that it is still possible to agree, but we are quite clear that we need to find solutions to all the outstanding issues, including civil society, in order to be able to reach an agreement with Hungary. So we don't split civil society up from the other funds – you could, for example. think that the funds were agreed, i.e. approx. 90 pst. first, and then come back to civil society. Everything has to be in place before we pay the funds. EEA funds for Hungary will therefore not be disbursed until there is a comprehensive agreement in place. In order to assist beneficiary countries in the development and implementation of agreed areas of activity, we have also established cooperation with Norwegian universities through the EEA funds, such as: The Administration of the Court of Justice and international organisations such as the Council of Europe and the European Agency for Fundamental Rights. This cooperation also contributes to further strengthening the focus on international standards, democracy and the rule of law. The core values of the Council of Europe – human rights, democracy and the rule of law – are more important than ever. Norway is a strong supporter of the organisation, and not least we support the European Court of Human Rights, both financially and professionally. We have increased our voluntary contributions to the court. We will continue to end with the organisation's monitoring mechanisms and the human rights commissioner's need for greater access to so-called grey areas in some Member States. Norway wants to preserve the Council of Europe as a pan-European organisation that includes all Europe’s 830 million inhabitants and gives them access to the Court of Human Rights. It's quite unique that we have a death penalty-free zone that includes 830 million people, and we want it to continue to be like that. The field work of the OSCE is also important. We have increased our involvement through personnel and project funding to the OSCE activities in our neighbourhoods south and east of Europe. We also support the OSCE's comprehensive election observations, and we contribute to the OSCE's important efforts for equality, media freedom, security sector reform and the prevention of violent extremism. The situation regarding human rights and the position of the rule of law in Turkey is still very worrying. The government has repeatedly expressed our concern in bilateral talks and through the Council of Europe and other appropriate channels. Governments have strengthened support for civil society in Turkey as the situation worsened following the 2016 coup trial. Actions for freedom of expression have the highest priority. From many quarters, there is concern about authoritarian developments and an increasingly difficult human rights situation in Russia. I share the concern about this development. This concerns both the conditions of civil society in general and specific issues such as the persecution of LHBTI individuals and the undue pressure against human rights defenders in Chechnya. Norway was one of 16 countries that activated the so-called Moscow mechanism in the OSCE. This included, inter alia: an expert review of reported human rights violations in Chechnya. Finally, as regards the establishment of human rights sanctions, Norway is now in dialogue with neighbouring countries on the ongoing process in the EU, where various issues related to the establishment of such a sanction regime are discussed. There are many and sometimes difficult questions to address, but they are now being discussed in the EU, and we are taking part in that discussion. The Ministry of Foreign Affairs assesses how Norway can make the best possible contribution to these discussions and is already reviewing the current law on international sanctions and restrictive measures, so that any sanctions regime against persons for serious human rights violations can be implemented in Norwegian law.

Proposal No. 18 sounds: ‘The government is asking for the setting up of a tax and tax committee to present concrete proposals for a comprehensive re-conversion of the current tax system with the aim of contributing to Norway’s achievement of its national Paris objectives. Overall, the proposals shall also contribute to the reduction of economic inequality in the Norwegian society.’» Proposal No. 19 reads: ‘The government is asking how a model of climate reward can best be introduced in Norway. [[carbon charge for distribution]] It is assumed that, in addition to defining the sectors to be covered, the government also indicates the progressiveness of the tax rates necessary to achieve the climate objectives set out in the Paris Agreement.» Proposal No. 20 sounds: ‘The government is asked to assess a support scheme for shorter working hours in municipal jobs, in particular in burdensome professions such as care professionals.’
